			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Reduce RAG costs on Amazon Bedrock with query-aware compression</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/reduce-rag-costs-on-amazon-bedrock-with-query-aware-compression/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Aakanksha Veesam]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 21 Aug 2026 16:59:15 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Technical How-to]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">c9af9a1d9e8740d86e0066f2fec69fedabf450da</guid>

					<description>Input tokens are often a meaningful part of the cost of running Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG) at scale. This post describes a query-aware context compression pattern on Amazon Bedrock: after retrieval, a smaller model filters retrieved chunks against the query before the primary model answers, reducing input tokens and cost while preserving answer quality.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;Input tokens sent to the foundation model (FM) on every call are often a meaningful part of the cost of running &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/what-is/retrieval-augmented-generation/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Retrieval Augmented Generation (RAG)&lt;/a&gt; at scale. Query-aware compression offers one way to reduce how many of them reach the model. &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/?trk=7ecf60df-6136-414c-a7c3-6aa4d2d6019f&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=EAIaIQobChMIypH5r4fBlQMVqEx_AB1OvwmNEAAYASAAEgIGTfD_BwE&amp;amp;gads_camp=23532472972&amp;amp;gads_ag=194311072004&amp;amp;gads_ad=795877020842&amp;amp;gads_kw=amazon%20bedrock&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=9026933&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23532472972&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp8ByFw1VLhJ9bdURylYSJ7dd&amp;amp;gclid=EAIaIQobChMIypH5r4fBlQMVqEx_AB1OvwmNEAAYASAAEgIGTfD_BwE" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; provides the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/what-is/foundation-models/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;foundation models&lt;/a&gt; and features to build RAG applications. RAG retrieval usually tunes for high recall, returning a broad set of potentially relevant chunks so the primary model has thorough source material to work with. This design helps builders feel confident that the right information is available at inference time. As workloads scale, builders often look for ways to optimize the cost-performance tradeoff by reducing the number of input tokens the primary model processes while maintaining answer quality. The open, composable architecture of Amazon Bedrock supports custom post-retrieval processing steps that refine what reaches the primary model.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we describe a post-retrieval customization pattern that achieves significant input-token reduction, and therefore cost savings, while preserving answer quality. It’s compatible with RAG retrievers on Amazon Bedrock, including Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/knowledge-bases/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Knowledge Bases&lt;/a&gt;. As a secondary benefit, removing irrelevant context reduces the surface area for hallucination. After retrieval but before the final answer call, a smaller, lower-cost model on Amazon Bedrock filters retrieved chunks against the user’s query. The primary model then receives the filtered context and generates the answer.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;We cover the pattern’s architecture at a high level, show the core Amazon Bedrock implementation in a &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/pm/lambda/?trk=2abe6167-e3db-40c4-a9fa-b283e7b4d7c8&amp;amp;sc_channel=ps&amp;amp;ef_id=EAIaIQobChMI0eez9oXBlQMVVTfUAR2_2xb9EAAYASAAEgI-OvD_BwE&amp;amp;gads_camp=23527793912&amp;amp;gads_ag=191938386622&amp;amp;gads_ad=802094701896&amp;amp;gads_kw=amazon%20lambda&amp;amp;gads_matchtype=e&amp;amp;gads_network=g&amp;amp;gads_device=c&amp;amp;gads_geo=9026933&amp;amp;gad_campaignid=23527793912&amp;amp;gbraid=0AAAAADjHtp8GsQ-cK5F5U0jJWeu2our88&amp;amp;gclid=EAIaIQobChMI0eez9oXBlQMVVTfUAR2_2xb9EAAYASAAEgI-OvD_BwE" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Lambda&lt;/a&gt; function, walk through the cost model and the latency tradeoff, and describe how we evaluated answer quality. We also look at how this pattern can layer on top of existing Amazon Bedrock capabilities like &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/prompt-caching.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;prompt caching&lt;/a&gt;, Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/intelligent-prompt-routing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Intelligent Prompt Routing&lt;/a&gt;, and the Rerank API for compounding cost savings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="prerequisites"&gt;Prerequisites&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To implement the solution, complete the following prerequisite steps:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Have an active &lt;a href="https://signin.aws.amazon.com/signin?redirect_uri=https%3A%2F%2Fportal.aws.amazon.com%2Fbilling%2Fsignup%2Fresume&amp;amp;client_id=signup" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS account&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Create an &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/iam/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Identity and Access Management&lt;/a&gt; (IAM) role for the Lambda function to access Amazon Bedrock. For instructions, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/IAM/latest/UserGuide/id_roles_create_for-service.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Create a role to delegate permissions to an AWS service&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Add policy permissions to the IAM role.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Amazon Bedrock model access in your AWS Region for the two models in the cascade: a smaller compression model (Anthropic Claude Haiku in this post) and a primary model (Anthropic Claude Sonnet). &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/model-access.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Request access&lt;/a&gt; on the Amazon Bedrock console under Model access.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="how-query-aware-compression-reduces-rag-costs-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;How query-aware compression reduces RAG costs on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A RAG flow using traditional RAG infrastructure or frameworks looks like:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The application embeds the user query.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The vector index returns the top-k chunks (commonly 5–20).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Optionally, a reranker reorders those chunks by relevance.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;All retrieved chunks are concatenated into the prompt.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The primary model generates the answer.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Retrieved context scales with top-k and chunk size: retrieving 5–20 chunks at typical chunk sizes puts many technical-documentation and legal RAG workloads in the range of several thousand input tokens per query. Reducing the per-query token count can yield meaningful cost savings.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="architecture-overview"&gt;Architecture overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A smaller model reads the retrieved chunks alongside the query and outputs only the verbatim spans relevant to the question. We use Claude Haiku in this post, but the pattern works with other small/primary model pairs within a model family on Amazon Bedrock. Both the compression call and the primary model’s answer call run inside a single AWS Lambda function. Upstream, a retriever embeds the query and returns the top-k chunks. An Amazon Bedrock knowledge base, the fully managed RAG capability backed by Amazon OpenSearch Serverless, is one such retriever. The Lambda function receives those chunks as input and returns the final answer. The compression call is the only step added to a standard RAG flow.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Because the smaller model costs less per token than the primary model, trimming the context before the expensive answer call is where the savings come from. How large those savings are comes down to two things.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram shows the solution architecture.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/19/ML-21148-1.png" alt="Query-aware compression architecture: a retriever returns top-k chunks to a Lambda function, a smaller model compresses them, then the primary model generates the answer" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Query-aware context compression architecture on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;The flow proceeds through the following steps:&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ol type="1"&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The user submits a query to the application.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The application sends the query to the retriever, which searches the vectorized knowledge sources.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The retriever returns the top-k chunks (full retrieved context) to the application.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The application passes the query and all retrieved chunks to an AWS Lambda function, which sends them to a smaller model (Claude Haiku) through the Amazon Bedrock Converse API. This is the compression call.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The smaller model outputs only the verbatim spans relevant to the query (the compressed context).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The Lambda function sends the query and the compressed context to the primary model (Claude Sonnet) through the Converse API. This is the answer call.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The primary model generates the final answer, which the application returns to the user.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ol&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="why-this-works-economically"&gt;Why this works economically&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The economics depend on two factors: the price ratio between the small and primary models on Amazon Bedrock, and the compression ratio the smaller model achieves.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For a single RAG query with &lt;code&gt;R&lt;/code&gt; retrieved input tokens, a compression ratio of &lt;code&gt;c&lt;/code&gt; (where &lt;code&gt;c&lt;/code&gt; &amp;gt; 1), a final answer output of &lt;code&gt;A&lt;/code&gt; tokens, and per-token prices &lt;code&gt;P_small_in&lt;/code&gt; / &lt;code&gt;P_small_out&lt;/code&gt; (smaller model input and output price) and &lt;code&gt;P_large_in&lt;/code&gt; / &lt;code&gt;P_large_out&lt;/code&gt; (primary model input and output price):&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&amp;nbsp;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Stage&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Baseline&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;With compression&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;What happens&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Smaller model reads chunks&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;—&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;R&lt;/code&gt; × &lt;code&gt;P_small_in&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The smaller model receives the query and all retrieved chunks as input&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Smaller model writes compressed output&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;—&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;(R/c)&lt;/code&gt; × &lt;code&gt;P_small_out&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The smaller model outputs only the verbatim spans relevant to the query&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Primary model reads context&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;R&lt;/code&gt; × &lt;code&gt;P_large_in&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;(R/c)&lt;/code&gt; × &lt;code&gt;P_large_in&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The primary model receives the context as input to generate the answer&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Primary model writes answer&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;A&lt;/code&gt; × &lt;code&gt;P_large_out&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;A&lt;/code&gt; × &lt;code&gt;P_large_out&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The primary model generates the final answer for the user&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Total&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;R·P_large_in + A·P_large_out&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;R·P_small_in + (R/c)·P_small_out + (R/c)·P_large_in + A·P_large_out&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The compression call adds the input and output cost of the smaller model. Savings come from sending &lt;code&gt;R/c&lt;/code&gt; instead of &lt;code&gt;R&lt;/code&gt; tokens to the primary model. The economics favor compression when:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Retrieved context is large (the savings on the larger model dominate).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The price ratio between large and small is high (Sonnet/Opus paired with Haiku).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A meaningful portion of the retrieved content can be trimmed for a given query without affecting answer quality.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="implementation-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;Implementation on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern fits between retrieval and the final answer call. We implement it as a single AWS Lambda function that orchestrates the two Amazon Bedrock model invocations using the Converse API. The function receives the user query and the retrieved chunks as its input event.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-compression-prompt"&gt;The compression prompt&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The compression prompt is the most important part of the implementation. It must instruct the smaller model to extract spans rather than summarize, forbid paraphrasing and rewriting, and preserve enough surrounding context for citations to remain accurate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;COMPRESSION_SYSTEM_PROMPT = """You extract evidence from retrieved documents.
You will receive a user QUESTION and a list of CHUNKS.

Your job:
1. For each chunk, identify the spans (verbatim sentences or short paragraphs)
that contain evidence directly relevant to answering the QUESTION.
2. Output ONLY those spans, copied verbatim from the source. Do not paraphrase,
summarize, or rewrite.
3. Preserve chunk identifiers so the downstream system can cite sources.
4. If a chunk contains no relevant evidence, output the chunk identifier
followed by NO_RELEVANT_EVIDENCE.

Output format (strict):
[CHUNK_ID: &amp;lt;id&amp;gt;]
&amp;lt;verbatim span 1&amp;gt;
&amp;lt;verbatim span 2&amp;gt;
[CHUNK_ID: &amp;lt;id&amp;gt;]
NO_RELEVANT_EVIDENCE

Do not add commentary, headings, conclusions, or your own words. Only verbatim
spans from the source chunks, grouped by chunk identifier."""&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="the-lambda-function"&gt;The Lambda function&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The function takes the user query and the retrieved chunks, reads the two model IDs from environment variables, and initializes an Amazon Bedrock Runtime client configured with adaptive retries. It then makes two calls through the Bedrock Converse API: the first to the smaller model to compress the chunks, and the second to the primary model to generate the answer from the compressed evidence. The compression call runs at temperature 0.0, which keeps the extraction deterministic so the smaller model copies spans as they appear in the source:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Step 1: Compress. The smaller model filters the retrieved chunks.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;compressed = bedrock.converse(
    modelId=COMPRESSION_MODEL_ID,
    system=[{"text": COMPRESSION_SYSTEM_PROMPT}],
    messages=[{"role": "user",
               "content": [{"text": f"QUESTION:\n{query}\n\nCHUNKS:\n{chunks}"}]}],
    inferenceConfig={"temperature": 0.0},
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Step 2: Answer. The primary model reasons over the filtered evidence.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div class="hide-language"&gt; 
 &lt;pre&gt;&lt;code class="language-python"&gt;answer = bedrock.converse(
    modelId=ANSWER_MODEL_ID,
    system=[{"text": "Answer using only the evidence provided. Cite sources."}],
    messages=[{"role": "user",
               "content": [{"text": f"QUESTION:\n{query}\n\nEVIDENCE:\n{compressed}"}]}],
)&lt;/code&gt;&lt;/pre&gt; 
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Note:&lt;/strong&gt; The prompts are an example and should be adapted to your documents and question types.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="methodology"&gt;Methodology&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Before recommending this pattern, we evaluated it empirically. The benchmark covered:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A corpus of more than 500,000 documents spanning 9 enterprise source types, including chat messages, email, issue-tracker tickets, shared-drive documents, CRM records, meeting transcripts, code repositories, and wiki pages.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A set of 500 questions across 10 categories, ranging from narrow factual lookups to broader multi-part questions, including both precisely worded and more informally phrased queries.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Multiple compression conditions: no compression (baseline), compression, and rerank-plus-compression.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Each query run through baseline and optimized pipelines, with answers scored by a &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/what-is/large-language-model/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;large language model&lt;/a&gt; (LLM) judge.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Quality scoring across four dimensions (correctness, completeness, citation accuracy, and conciseness) against a reference answer. Faithfulness was tracked separately, checking each answer’s claims against the evidence the primary model received.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;These figures describe one corpus, one domain, and one query distribution. Results on your own documents and queries will differ.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="results"&gt;Results&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following table summarizes the headline results across the queries, comparing the baseline, compression, and rerank + compression pipelines.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Metric&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Baseline&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Compression&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Rerank + Compression&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Cost&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;100% (ref)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;67%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;64%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Tokens to model&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;100% (ref)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;12%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;10%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;+19% (slower)&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;+12% (slower)&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;100% (ref)&lt;/td&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;97.6%&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Hallucination rate&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;51%&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;−7 pts&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;−13 pts&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following figures come from the benchmark. Results on your own corpus, queries, and model choices will differ. The following figure shows the average of query cost saving (left axis, percentage versus baseline) and the reduction in context sent to the primary model (right axis, times fewer tokens). Compression achieved a 33 percent cost saving or 8.6× fewer tokens. Rerank + compression reached 36 percent cost saving and 10.1× fewer tokens.&lt;/p&gt;

Completeness and citation accuracy are slightly lower under compression, while conciseness is slightly higher.&lt;/p&gt;

&lt;/div&gt;

&lt;/div&gt;

 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/19/ML-21148-5.png" alt="Bar chart of cost savings for the typical-query and hard-query sets across the compression and rerank plus compression pipelines" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 5: Cost savings for the typical-query and hard-query sets&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="considerations-for-production-use"&gt;Considerations for production use&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Three things must be weighed before this pattern is adopted: the latency of the added compression call, the impact on answer quality, and whether the workload is a fit. Each is covered in the following sections.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="latency"&gt;Latency&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Adding a smaller-model call introduces one extra step in the path. Claude Haiku is optimized for speed, and because the primary model then processes a smaller, focused context, part of that added time is recovered on the answer call. The total end-to-end latency is the compression call plus the answer call on the focused context. Net impact depends on how compute-bound the primary model is on the original context size.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For latency-critical surfaces (sub-second chat), measure with your own context sizes before deploying.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="quality"&gt;Quality&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Compression involves a few factors worth deliberate engineering.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;table border="1px" width="100%" cellpadding="10px"&gt; 
 &lt;tbody&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Dimension&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Design consideration&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;strong&gt;How the pattern handles it&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Factual consistency&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The primary model should receive all the evidence it needs&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The prompt instructs faithful, verbatim extraction of all query-relevant evidence&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Citation preservation&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Spans stay tied to their source chunk&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;[CHUNK_ID: &amp;lt;id&amp;gt;]&lt;/code&gt; markers carried through the prompt&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Faithful extraction&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Output stays verbatim from the source&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;&lt;code&gt;temperature=0.0&lt;/code&gt;, “verbatim only” instruction, optional post-hoc span validation&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Multi-step reasoning&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Evidence chains stay intact for analytical questions&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The compression model preserves more spans when the question draws on multiple sources&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Numerical reasoning&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Specific numbers are retained&lt;/td&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;The “verbatim only” instruction preserves numbers and calculations as they appear in the source&lt;/td&gt; 
  &lt;/tr&gt; 
 &lt;/tbody&gt; 
&lt;/table&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="choosing-the-smaller-model"&gt;Choosing the smaller model&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;A few considerations can help guide the selection of the smaller model:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Relative pricing.&lt;/strong&gt; The savings of this pattern depend on the price ratio between the smaller model and the primary model. A lower relative price for the smaller model leaves more room for net savings once the cost of the compression call is included. Amazon Bedrock model families span a range of price points, with current rates available on the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing&lt;/a&gt; page.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Inference speed.&lt;/strong&gt; The compression call is added to the request path, so a smaller model optimized for fast inference limits the additional latency.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Model-family alignment.&lt;/strong&gt; Selecting the smaller and primary models from the same family keeps formatting, chunk markers, and instruction handling consistent across both calls. Claude Haiku with Claude Sonnet, and Amazon Nova Micro with Amazon Nova Pro, are examples of such pairings on Amazon Bedrock. For model availability by Region, refer to &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/models-regions.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Supported models by AWS Region in Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="recommended-evaluation-approach-before-deploying"&gt;Recommended evaluation approach before deploying&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Assemble an evaluation set of representative queries from your application logs (de-identified), covering both narrow lookups and broader multi-fact questions in the proportion they occur in production.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Run the baseline and compressed pipelines on the same retrieval results, so the comparison reflects only the effect of compression.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Score both sets of answers on the dimensions that matter for the workload, such as correctness, completeness, and citation accuracy against a reference answer, along with faithfulness against the evidence the primary model received, which surfaces claims not supported by that evidence.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Set acceptance thresholds that fit the workload, and promote the configuration after it meets them.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;For production deployments, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/guardrails/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock Guardrails&lt;/a&gt; can provide additional content filtering and grounding validation as complementary controls alongside the compression pattern.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="where-this-pattern-fits-well"&gt;Where this pattern fits well&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern provides value when three conditions line up. Retrieved context is large, the primary model is the expensive part of the call, and the question is narrow relative to the breadth of what was retrieved. The following scenarios share these characteristics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Regulated-industry compliance and policy assistants.&lt;/strong&gt; Utilities, financial services, insurance, and healthcare keep long bulletins, tariffs, and policy documents where a single section answers most questions. Retrieval pulls whole bulletins (8,000–15,000 tokens) but the analyst needs one clause. Verbatim extraction preserves the exact regulatory wording and the source ID, which matters when the answer has to be defensible in an audit.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Customer-support copilots over a large product knowledge base.&lt;/strong&gt; Top-k is set high so the assistant is less likely to miss an edge case, meaning most tickets retrieve far more than they need. A “how do I reset X” or “what’s the fee for Y” ticket usually resolves from one or two paragraphs buried in a long article. Compression cuts the per-ticket inference cost without touching the retrieval strategy support engineers already trust. At support volumes (hundreds of thousands of tickets a month), small per-query savings accumulate quickly.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Internal engineering and operations assistants over runbooks and wikis.&lt;/strong&gt; Confluence, SharePoint, and runbook RAG retrieves large chunks because internal docs are verbose and loosely structured. Questions are usually specific (“which IAM role does service Z assume”, “what’s the rollback step for migration N”). The answer is a few lines. The surrounding policy and background is not needed for the response.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Financial research and earnings-call analysis.&lt;/strong&gt; Retrieved chunks are dense (10-K sections, transcript paragraphs) and questions are pointed (“what did the CFO say about FX exposure in Q3”). Compression preserves the analyst’s ability to quote the source verbatim while reducing the per-query cost on the larger model.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This pattern is likely worth a prototype if most of these are true for your workload:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Average retrieved context per query exceeds roughly 5,000 tokens.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;The primary model’s input tokens are expensive enough that a 3–10× reduction in context is material.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Most questions are narrow relative to the volume retrieved, rather than broad “summarize everything” requests.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;Your latency budget can absorb one extra model call (a few hundred milliseconds to approximately 1 second).&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;You have, or can build, an evaluation set to compare quality before and after.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;If your workload is sub-second conversational chat with small retrieved context, this pattern may not be the best fit. Consider Amazon Bedrock features like prompt caching and Intelligent Prompt Routing, which provide more value with less added latency.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The pattern fits into RAG pipelines built on Amazon Bedrock: A single Lambda function between retrieval and the final model call compresses context and fine-tunes cost and quality for your specific workload.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The compression prompt handles both simple lookups and complex multi-source questions without requiring separate routing logic. Combined with existing features like prompt caching, Intelligent Prompt Routing, and the Rerank API, this approach delivers compounding optimizations across the full RAG pipeline.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;You can use this pattern behind a feature flag, measure it on your real query distribution, and tune the compression prompts to match your domain.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To get started, refer to the &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock documentation&lt;/a&gt; and the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/pricing/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock pricing page&lt;/a&gt; for current model costs.&lt;/p&gt;

			</item>
		<item>
		<title>Accelerating aircraft IFEC diagnostics with agentic AI on AWS</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/accelerating-aircraft-ifec-diagnostics-with-agentic-ai-on-aws/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Satyen Yadav]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Fri, 21 Aug 2026 16:57:01 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Advanced (300)]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Amazon SageMaker]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">ed00b717fca4a544868c35ad4ab20128af9b7c97</guid>

					<description>Panasonic Avionics worked with AWS and the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center to build an agentic AI system on Amazon Bedrock, Amazon SageMaker, and AWS Glue that diagnoses in-flight entertainment and connectivity (IFEC) issues across a global fleet, reducing diagnosis time from hours to minutes while maintaining accuracy.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://www.panasonic.aero/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Panasonic Avionics Corporation&lt;/a&gt; provides in-flight entertainment and connectivity (IFEC) systems across a large global fleet serving hundreds of airlines and billions of passengers annually. When a system issue affects passenger experience at this scale, engineers must diagnose the root cause quickly across thousands of unique deployment configurations. Doing this manually, correlating logs, metrics, and ticketing data across diverse fleet variants, can take hours and requires deep institutional knowledge.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, you will learn how Panasonic Avionics Corporation worked with AWS and the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center for architectural guidance to build an agentic AI system. The solution uses &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue&lt;/a&gt; to significantly reduce diagnosis time while maintaining high accuracy.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="challenge-evolving-maintenance-at-scale"&gt;Challenge: Evolving maintenance at scale&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Panasonic Avionics Corporation’s operational data serves as a critical asset for monitoring fleet IFEC health. The company relies on a data lake and data system built on AWS to store and organize operational data gathered from across its fleet, processing large volumes of data daily.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Despite this robust data infrastructure, translating raw operational data into actionable diagnostics presented operational challenges at scale. Panasonic Avionics Corporation deploys services with configurations tailored to individual operational requirements. Each deployment generates unique log patterns, which complicates fleet-wide performance assessment. Teams performed manual reviews to correlate metrics across multiple operational data sources.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This process presented several opportunities for optimization across operational efficiency and engineering productivity:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Manual analysis effort&lt;/strong&gt; – Correlation across diverse configurations required detailed manual investigation. This facilitated diagnostic accuracy but extended the overall analysis cycle.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mean Time to Detect (MTTD)&lt;/strong&gt; – Detection relied primarily on ticket generation and manual review, which influenced how quickly emerging patterns were identified.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Mean Time to Resolve (MTTR)&lt;/strong&gt; – Resolution timelines included significant investigative effort before corrective action could begin, contributing to longer end-to-end resolution cycles.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Automation opportunities&lt;/strong&gt; – Repetitive investigative tasks and log reviews created significant opportunities for intelligent automation, particularly during peak activity periods.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Knowledge scaling&lt;/strong&gt; – Manual correlation processes required deep system familiarity, creating opportunities to use AI to scale institutional knowledge more broadly across engineering teams.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Resource optimization&lt;/strong&gt; – Engineers spent recurring time on diagnostic activities, reducing bandwidth for innovation, feature development, and long-term reliability improvements.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Panasonic Avionics Corporation identified a clear opportunity to evolve toward proactive health monitoring and fleet-wide pattern recognition, while preserving diagnostic rigor. The objective was to maintain analytical depth while significantly improving MTTD and MTTR, so engineers can focus more on solution design, optimization, and strategic reliability enhancements rather than investigative overhead.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="solution-overview"&gt;Solution overview&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Panasonic Avionics Corporation partnered with AWS and the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center to explore how generative AI could enhance internal diagnostic workflows across its fleet IFEC operations. The collaboration combined Panasonic Avionics Corporation’s deep domain expertise in IFEC operations with generative AI capabilities available through AWS services and the Innovation Center’s architectural guidance on system design.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="architecture"&gt;Architecture&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following diagram illustrates the diagnostic architecture on AWS. The solution uses a multi-agent workflow system that processes data through three distinct layers working in harmony. A &lt;em&gt;Trend Analyzer&lt;/em&gt; identifies anomalies by analyzing key performance indicators and service degradation metrics. &lt;em&gt;Parallel Diagnostic Agents&lt;/em&gt; execute specific diagnostic functions including correlation analysis, system checks, and log pattern matching. A &lt;em&gt;Summarizer&lt;/em&gt; powered by a large language model (LLM) integrates outputs into coherent diagnostic reports with root cause analysis and recommended actions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;div style="width: 810px" class="wp-caption alignnone"&gt;
 &lt;img src="https://d2908q01vomqb2.cloudfront.net/f1f836cb4ea6efb2a0b1b99f41ad8b103eff4b59/2026/08/18/ML-20350-1.png" alt="Multi-agent diagnostic workflow: Trend Analyzer, parallel Diagnostic Agents, and an LLM Summarizer processing fleet data on AWS" width="800"&gt;
 &lt;p class="wp-caption-text"&gt;&lt;br&gt;Figure 1: Multi-agent IFEC diagnostic architecture on AWS&lt;/p&gt;
&lt;/div&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="solution-walkthrough"&gt;Solution walkthrough&lt;/h3&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system processes operational data through the following five phases:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;1. Ingest and normalize&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system ingests raw operational data from across the fleet, transforms it into standardized service metrics, and stores it in an Amazon Simple Storage Service (Amazon S3) data lakehouse using Apache Iceberg. AWS Glue and Amazon EMR handle this extract, transform, and load (ETL) pipeline. A domain ontology, a shared vocabulary that defines fleet entities and their relationships, normalizes terminology across fleet variants. This consistency lets data from diverse configurations be compared at fleet scale. The ontology also links performance metrics with configuration metadata and ticketing information, creating a unified view for cross-fleet diagnostics.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;2. Detect&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The Trend Analyzer agent continuously evaluates key performance indicators, service-level adherence, and degradation metrics. Fleet-wide relationship modeling detects patterns invisible when examining individual deployments in isolation, such as gradual degradation affecting only deployments sharing a specific configuration variant.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;This moves Panasonic Avionics Corporation from reactive detection through ticket generation to proactive identification of emerging problems before they escalate.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;3. Diagnose&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;When the Trend Analyzer flags a concern, parallel diagnostic agents investigate simultaneously from multiple angles:&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Correlation Analyzer:&lt;/strong&gt; Detects recurring patterns across deployments that share configurations, identifying whether an issue is isolated or systemic.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;System Checks:&lt;/strong&gt; Validates metadata and service status against ticketing workflows, determining whether known maintenance activities explain observed behavior.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Log Analyzer:&lt;/strong&gt; Matches current log patterns against a library of previously identified failure modes using rule-based and pattern-based detection.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon SageMaker orchestrates these agents using LangGraph, an open source framework for managing stateful AI workflows. The Strands Agents SDK, an open source Python framework for building AI agents, provides the agent implementation and execution capabilities. Together, they run these agents in parallel, which reduces investigation from hours of manual review to minutes of automated analysis in Panasonic Avionics Corporation’s internal testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;4. Contextualize&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system queries past incidents and resolution artifacts stored as vector representations in Amazon Relational Database Service (Amazon RDS) with pgvector (a PostgreSQL extension for vector similarity search). Semantic search retrieves similar patterns and their resolutions, even when exact symptoms differ, giving the system institutional memory that scales across the engineering organization.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;5. Recommend and act&lt;/strong&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Anthropic Claude on Amazon Bedrock synthesizes findings from the Correlation Analyzer, System Checks, and Log Analyzer into structured diagnostic reports. Each report includes root cause hypotheses, impact analysis across affected fleet segments, and prioritized remediation recommendations.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The system categorizes reports by severity. For critical findings, the system automatically creates alerts, prioritizes incidents, and routes them to the appropriate engineering teams with recommended resolution actions, alleviating manual triage while preserving engineering oversight for remediation decisions.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;AI-generated recommendations are grounded using retrieved operational data and historical incidents, validated against deterministic business rules, and presented with supporting evidence. Human engineers review diagnostic findings and approve remediation actions for operationally significant incidents. The system also maintains traceability of agent decisions and recommendations to support auditability and continuous improvement.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Across these five phases, building a fleet-scale diagnostic system required infrastructure that grows with demand. Serverless data processing through AWS Glue and Amazon EMR, the model flexibility of Amazon Bedrock, and the governed agent runtime of Amazon SageMaker let Panasonic Avionics Corporation focus on diagnostic logic rather than infrastructure.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="from-concept-to-production"&gt;From concept to production&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Panasonic Avionics Corporation validated the solution with guidance from the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center advisory team on event-driven design patterns, parallel processing strategies, and performance optimization techniques for production scalability.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Establish rigorous validation early:&lt;/strong&gt; Panasonic Avionics Corporation implemented cross-reference validation against ground truth data, achieving accuracy that consistently exceeded requirements throughout testing.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Design for modularity and transparency:&lt;/strong&gt; Design principles emphasized atomic agent operations, transparency in prompts and logic, and human-in-the-loop review capabilities. This modular approach allowed for independent agent updates without requiring system-wide changes as the solution scales.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Optimize AI components strategically:&lt;/strong&gt; Guidance emphasized using tuned parameter configurations for deterministic outputs and focused responses. The team limited LLM use to summarization and error reasoning tasks where generative capabilities add clear value.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;strong&gt;Iterate with continuous feedback:&lt;/strong&gt; The solution moved to production only when automated actions aligned with manual expert assessment, so the system maintained diagnostic accuracy while reducing time to resolution. The system now generates diagnostic reports daily covering the active fleet.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="conclusion"&gt;Conclusion&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Panasonic Avionics Corporation’s AI-driven diagnostic system on AWS delivers measurable improvements across key operational metrics. Manual analysis effort decreased significantly, while Mean Time to Detect (MTTD) and Mean Time to Resolve (MTTR) both improved. In targeted use cases, the system has demonstrated 20–40 percent improvements in operational efficiency. Support teams confirmed significant reduction in repetitive investigative burden, as the system now handles work that previously required hours of manual effort. Engineering capacity for independent diagnostics increased meaningfully, so the team can diagnose and resolve issues that previously required escalation. These improvements fundamentally shift the operational model as systems grow, so the team can handle increased scale through AI agents and reduce both costs and operational risk.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;To explore how the AWS Generative AI Innovation Center can help your organization build similar solutions, visit the &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/generative-ai/innovation-center/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Generative AI Innovation Center&lt;/a&gt;. For more on the services used in this post, refer to &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt;, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/sagemaker/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon SageMaker&lt;/a&gt;, and &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/glue/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS Glue&lt;/a&gt;. For aviation-specific use cases, visit &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/aerospace-and-satellite/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;AWS for Aerospace and Satellite&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/p&gt;

 &lt;li&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/build-a-serverless-conversational-ai-agent-using-claude-with-langgraph-and-managed-mlflow-on-amazon-sagemaker-ai/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Build a serverless conversational AI agent using Claude with LangGraph and managed MLflow on Amazon SageMaker AI&lt;/a&gt;.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt;

Actual results might vary based on data characteristics, operational context, and system configuration.&lt;/p&gt;

		<item>
		<title>Introducing cross-Region inference for OpenAI GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock</title>
		<link>https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/introducing-cross-region-inference-for-openai-gpt-5-6-models-on-amazon-bedrock/</link>
		
		<dc:creator><![CDATA[Melanie Li]]></dc:creator>
		<pubDate>Thu, 20 Aug 2026 21:46:03 +0000</pubDate>
				<category><![CDATA[Amazon Bedrock]]></category>
		<category><![CDATA[Announcements]]></category>
		<guid isPermaLink="false">95e4972674e71ff3cec032accfd9935a8cacf472</guid>

					<description>Amazon Bedrock now offers OpenAI GPT-5.6 models (Sol, Terra, and Luna) in more than 25 AWS Regions with cross-Region inference. Learn how US geographic and global inference profiles route requests for higher throughput, how to call the models with the OpenAI and Converse APIs, and how to configure IAM, quotas, and monitoring.</description>
										<content:encoded>&lt;p&gt;&lt;em&gt;This post is co-written with Chris Dickens from OpenAI.&lt;/em&gt;&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;&lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/bedrock/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;Amazon Bedrock&lt;/a&gt; now offers OpenAI GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock in more than 25 AWS Regions, with cross-Region inference. Three GPT-5.6 variants support cross-Region inference, Sol, Terra, and Luna, each tuned for a different balance of capability and cost.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Cross-Region inference (CRIS) in Amazon Bedrock works through inference profiles, which define a model and the AWS Regions to which Amazon Bedrock can route a request. You call the profile from a source Region, and Amazon Bedrock routes the request to a destination Region using compute in that Region. CRIS is primarily a capacity mechanism. By allowing requests to draw on a broader pool of compute rather than being bound to one Region’s available capacity, it improves throughput and helps maintain consistent performance under load. A geographic inference profile routes requests within a single geography, so you can scale while keeping data processed within that geography. For GPT-5.6, &lt;a href="https://aws.amazon.com/about-aws/whats-new/2026/08/amazon-bedrock-cross-region-openai-v2/" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;this launch introduces US geographic (US cross-Region inference) and global CRIS&lt;/a&gt;. A global inference profile routes across all supported AWS commercial Regions where the model is deployed, based on real-time capacity, giving you broad access to Amazon Bedrock capacity.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;In this post, we walk through an overview of GPT-5.6 models on Amazon Bedrock, how geographic and global cross-Region inference work for these models, and how to call them from the Amazon Bedrock console and in code with the OpenAI Responses API, the OpenAI Chat Completions API, and the Amazon Bedrock Converse API.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h2 id="gpt-5.6-on-amazon-bedrock"&gt;GPT-5.6 on Amazon Bedrock&lt;/h2&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The GPT-5.6 family on Amazon Bedrock includes general-purpose and specialized cyber security variants. This post covers the three general-purpose variants that support cross-Region inference, Sol, Terra, and Luna. All three accept text and image inputs and return text, have a 1 million token context window, and support reasoning mode, server-side tool calling, and prompt caching. You can call them with the OpenAI Responses API, the OpenAI Chat Completions API, and the Amazon Bedrock Converse API. Streaming is supported through the Responses and Chat Completions API (&lt;code&gt;stream=True&lt;/code&gt;), and &lt;code&gt;ConverseStream&lt;/code&gt;.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Amazon Bedrock inference profiles are logical identifiers you pass instead of a raw model ID.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;ul&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;geographic inference profile&lt;/strong&gt; (prefixed with a geography code such as &lt;code&gt;us.&lt;/code&gt;, for example us.openai.gpt-5.6-terra) keeps inference processing within the Regions of its predefined geography. Requests enter through your source Region and can only be routed to destination Regions inside that geography, so workloads with data residency requirements can scale across Regions while staying within the boundary.&lt;/li&gt; 
 &lt;li&gt;A &lt;strong&gt;Global inference profile&lt;/strong&gt; (prefixed &lt;code&gt;global.&lt;/code&gt; such as global.openai.gpt-5.6-terra) can route a request to any supported commercial AWS Region where the model is deployed, based on real-time capacity. It offers the widest capacity pool and is the right choice when your workload has no geographic processing requirements.&lt;/li&gt; 
&lt;/ul&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;Billing and quota consumption are tracked against your account regardless of which backend Region handled the request, so a single spending and throughput picture still applies. Data processed through global CRIS may cross the Regions in that model’s eligible set. If your workload has data residency requirements that restrict processing to specific geographies, use the geographic profile for that geography (such as us.openai.gpt-5.6-terra) or a direct call to one Region instead of the global profile. The Amazon Bedrock &lt;a href="https://docs.aws.amazon.com/bedrock/latest/userguide/inference-profiles-support.html" target="_blank" rel="noopener"&gt;cross-Region inference documentation&lt;/a&gt; lists which Regions participate in each model’s global and geographic profile sets.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;p&gt;The following tables list the source Regions where you can invoke the GPT-5.6 inference profiles and the destination Regions where your requests can be processed. The same routing applies to all three variants: Sol, Terra, and Luna.&lt;/p&gt; 
&lt;h3 id="us-cross-region-inference-profile-us.openai.gpt-5.6-sol--terra--luna"&gt;US cross-Region inference profile (us.openai.gpt-5.6-sol / -terra / -luna)&lt;/h3&gt;

   &lt;td&gt;US East (Ohio) us-east-2&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Canada (Central) ca-central-1&lt;/td&gt;

  &lt;/tr&gt; 
  &lt;tr&gt; 
   &lt;td&gt;Canada West (Calgary) ca-west-1&lt;/td&gt;
